I used to wander through exhibits for hours, especially if they had something with texture or those little side rooms people skip—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But I just haven’t been able to make myself care enough to go, even though I know I’d normally want to.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I’ve been trying to pin it on something, because it would almost be easier if I could say, “Oh, it started after this happened.” But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nWork is always stressful, but it’s kind of always been that way. There wasn’t some big blowup or move or loss or anything like that that I can point to. It just sort of… crept in, I guess. Although I keep second-guessing myself, because then I’ll think, well, maybe I’m just not handling normal stress well anymore. But no, I can’t name one specific event.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. The clearest one I can remember was a while back, and it went on for… I want to say around five months? It wasn’t every single second, but most days I felt really low and tired and just not like myself.\n\nI remember thinking at the time that maybe I was just in a rut, because everyone says that, you know—“oh, it’s just a rough patch”—and I kept trying to treat it like that. I’d rearrange my apartment or buy a new notebook, which sounds silly, but I’m one of those people who thinks a fresh notebook will fix my life. But it didn’t really lift for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like, I’ve had moments where everything feels kind of pointless, but I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking life isn’t worth living.\n\nSorry, I’m trying to answer clearly. I guess I haven’t really sat there thinking those exact words. It’s more like I get stuck staring at my coffee in the morning and thinking, “What am I even doing?”—which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making a plan or wanting to actually do that.\n\nIt’s more that I get overwhelmed and then my brain starts doing that spiral thing, like when you have too many tabs open on a computer and one of them is playing music but you can’t find which one—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. Not serious-serious, I don’t think. I mean, I’m still showing up. I’m going to work, I’m getting the main things done, and at home I’m not just disappearing or anything.

But it didn’t really lift for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like, I’ve had moments where everything feels kind of pointless, but I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking life isn’t worth living.\n\nSorry, I’m trying to answer clearly. I guess I haven’t really sat there thinking those exact words. It’s more like I get stuck staring at my coffee in the morning and thinking, “What am I even doing?”—which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making a plan or wanting to actually do that.\n\nIt’s more that I get overwhelmed and then my brain starts doing that spiral thing, like when you have too many tabs open on a computer and one of them is playing music but you can’t find which one—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “I’m going to kill myself.” At least, not that I can recognize.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worried in a clear way, like I’m pacing around thinking one specific thing is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get kind of tense and then I start overthinking small things—emails, whether I sounded strange in a meeting, that kind of stuff. But honestly, I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being a person with a job and too much inbox. My inbox is ridiculous. I keep trying to color-code it, which never works because then I forget what the colors mean.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess some worry, but I’m not sure it’s a lot.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way during this current period?\n\nPatient: Um… this stretch? I think about a month, maybe. It kind of started with me being way more energized and, like, weirdly driven to get everything done, and then it shifted into this low feeling that’s been hanging around.\n\nTime gets slippery for me when I’m trying to look back—my calendar is mostly just meetings and reminders to buy cat food, which doesn’t help—but yeah, I’d say this whole up-then-down thing has been going on for about a month.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that part was pretty noticeable. I’d sleep maybe four hours, which normally would make me useless, but I’d get up feeling… fine, almost ready to go. Like I should start sending emails immediately, or sketching out event ideas, or suddenly deciding the kitchen cabinets needed reorganizing.\n\nWhich, actually, I did start doing at one point, and then I found three different half-used bags of rice, so apparently we’re a rice household now. But yes — less sleep than usual, and I still felt rested, or at least charged up.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually confident or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. At the time it felt completely reasonable, but looking back, I was acting like I had the master key to everyone’s problems.\n\nI was honestly convinced I could fix the whole department morale issue at work if people would just follow my plan, and also somehow fix our family routines at home—like meals, chores, mornings, everything. I had charts in my head. Well, not just in my head, actually, I started making one, and then got distracted because the printer jammed, which always makes me irrationally angry.\n\nBut yes, I felt more capable than usual. Like I could just… organize everyone into being better.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… apparently I was. I didn’t totally hear it that way at the time, you know? In my head I was just explaining things efficiently.\n\nBut my husband said I was talking really fast, and a couple coworkers told me I kept jumping in before they could finish. Like I was sort of barreling through the conversation. I remember thinking they were being a little slow, which sounds rude now.\n\nIt’s funny, because I usually try to be careful in meetings—manager brain, let everyone have space, all that—but during that stretch I guess I was… not doing that. They said they couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Not serious-serious, I don’t think. I mean, I’m still showing up. I’m going to work, I’m getting the main things done, and at home I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut… it has affected things. I’ve been slower making decisions at work, which is awkward as a manager because people are sort of waiting on me. And at home I’ve been less reliable with family plans — like I’ll say we’re going to do something and then I’m not really prepared, or I lose track of it.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from the art stuff I usually do, which maybe sounds unrelated, but it’s one of the things that keeps me connected to people. I haven’t been showing up for that as much. So, not like everything fell apart, but it’s definitely made me less… steady.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But it didn’t really lift for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like, I’ve had moments where everything feels kind of pointless, but I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking life isn’t worth living.\n\nSorry, I’m trying to answer clearly. I guess I haven’t really sat there thinking those exact words. It’s more like I get stuck staring at my coffee in the morning and thinking, “What am I even doing?”—which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making a plan or wanting to actually do that.\n\nIt’s more that I get overwhelmed and then my brain starts doing that spiral thing, like when you have too many tabs open on a computer and one of them is playing music but you can’t find which one—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “I’m going to kill myself.” At least, not that I can recognize.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worried in a clear way, like I’m pacing around thinking one specific thing is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get kind of tense and then I start overthinking small things—emails, whether I sounded strange in a meeting, that kind of stuff. But honestly, I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being a person with a job and too much inbox. My inbox is ridiculous. I keep trying to color-code it, which never works because then I forget what the colors mean.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess some worry, but I’m not sure it’s a lot.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way during this current period?\n\nPatient: Um… this stretch? I think about a month, maybe. It kind of started with me being way more energized and, like, weirdly driven to get everything done, and then it shifted into this low feeling that’s been hanging around.\n\nTime gets slippery for me when I’m trying to look back—my calendar is mostly just meetings and reminders to buy cat food, which doesn’t help—but yeah, I’d say this whole up-then-down thing has been going on for about a month.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that part was pretty noticeable. I’d sleep maybe four hours, which normally would make me useless, but I’d get up feeling… fine, almost ready to go. Like I should start sending emails immediately, or sketching out event ideas, or suddenly deciding the kitchen cabinets needed reorganizing.\n\nWhich, actually, I did start doing at one point, and then I found three different half-used bags of rice, so apparently we’re a rice household now. But yes — less sleep than usual, and I still felt rested, or at least charged up.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually confident or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. At the time it felt completely reasonable, but looking back, I was acting like I had the master key to everyone’s problems.\n\nI was honestly convinced I could fix the whole department morale issue at work if people would just follow my plan, and also somehow fix our family routines at home—like meals, chores, mornings, everything. I had charts in my head. Well, not just in my head, actually, I started making one, and then got distracted because the printer jammed, which always makes me irrationally angry.\n\nBut yes, I felt more capable than usual. Like I could just… organize everyone into being better.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… apparently I was. I didn’t totally hear it that way at the time, you know? In my head I was just explaining things efficiently.\n\nBut my husband said I was talking really fast, and a couple coworkers told me I kept jumping in before they could finish. Like I was sort of barreling through the conversation. I remember thinking they were being a little slow, which sounds rude now.\n\nIt’s funny, because I usually try to be careful in meetings—manager brain, let everyone have space, all that—but during that stretch I guess I was… not doing that. They said they couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Not serious-serious, I don’t think. I mean, I’m still showing up. I’m going to work, I’m getting the main things done, and at home I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut… it has affected things. I’ve been slower making decisions at work, which is awkward as a manager because people are sort of waiting on me. And at home I’ve been less reliable with family plans — like I’ll say we’re going to do something and then I’m not really prepared, or I lose track of it.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from the art stuff I usually do, which maybe sounds unrelated, but it’s one of the things that keeps me connected to people. I haven’t been showing up for that as much. So, not like everything fell apart, but it’s definitely made me less… steady.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that energized period last?\n\nPatient: The energized part… I’m trying to count it cleanly. It wasn’t just one weekend or something. The whole swing, from being really driven and keyed up into being low, has been about a month now.\n\nThe energized stretch itself felt like it ran for days in a row—maybe I’d need to look at my calendar to be exact, because I always think I’ll remember and then I don’t. My calendar is a mess, too, which is funny because I keep telling everyone else to use shared calendars. But overall, this has been going on around a month.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized period last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… I mean, I’m not great at pinning down the exact start and stop. It kind of blended together.\n\nBut this whole recent swing — from being unusually energized and driven and then dropping into feeling low — has been going on for about a month. The energized part was definitely more than just a day or two. I’d have to check my calendar to be precise, though, assuming I actually wrote it down, which… lately has been questionable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’d have to check my calendar to be precise, though, assuming I actually wrote it down, which… lately has been questionable.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that energized period, did you have any hallucinations or delusional beliefs?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… I mean, I didn’t see or hear things that weren’t there, if that’s what you mean.

Delusional beliefs is harder for me to answer, because when I’m in that mode I can be very convinced my way of seeing things is the right one — but that’s also sort of my manager brain, you know? Like I can get overly sure about a plan or a decision, and then later I’m like, okay, maybe I was being a little much.

But not like… believing someone was spying on me or messages were being sent to me or anything like that. Nothing that extreme.

There wasn’t some big blowup or move or loss or anything like that that I can point to. It just sort of… crept in, I guess. Although I keep second-guessing myself, because then I’ll think, well, maybe I’m just not handling normal stress well anymore. But no, I can’t name one specific event.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. The clearest one I can remember was a while back, and it went on for… I want to say around five months? It wasn’t every single second, but most days I felt really low and tired and just not like myself.\n\nI remember thinking at the time that maybe I was just in a rut, because everyone says that, you know—“oh, it’s just a rough patch”—and I kept trying to treat it like that. I’d rearrange my apartment or buy a new notebook, which sounds silly, but I’m one of those people who thinks a fresh notebook will fix my life. But I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “I’m going to kill myself.” At least, not that I can recognize.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worried in a clear way, like I’m pacing around thinking one specific thing is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get kind of tense and then I start overthinking small things—emails, whether I sounded strange in a meeting, that kind of stuff. But honestly, I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being a person with a job and too much inbox. My inbox is ridiculous. I keep trying to color-code it, which never works because then I forget what the colors mean.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess some worry, but I’m not sure it’s a lot.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way during this current period?\n\nPatient: Um… this stretch? I think about a month, maybe. It kind of started with me being way more energized and, like, weirdly driven to get everything done, and then it shifted into this low feeling that’s been hanging around.\n\nTime gets slippery for me when I’m trying to look back—my calendar is mostly just meetings and reminders to buy cat food, which doesn’t help—but yeah, I’d say this whole up-then-down thing has been going on for about a month.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that part was pretty noticeable. I’d sleep maybe four hours, which normally would make me useless, but I’d get up feeling… fine, almost ready to go. Like I should start sending emails immediately, or sketching out event ideas, or suddenly deciding the kitchen cabinets needed reorganizing.\n\nWhich, actually, I did start doing at one point, and then I found three different half-used bags of rice, so apparently we’re a rice household now. Like I should start sending emails immediately, or sketching out event ideas, or suddenly deciding the kitchen cabinets needed reorganizing.\n\nWhich, actually, I did start doing at one point, and then I found three different half-used bags of rice, so apparently we’re a rice household now. But yes — less sleep than usual, and I still felt rested, or at least charged up.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually confident or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. At the time it felt completely reasonable, but looking back, I was acting like I had the master key to everyone’s problems.\n\nI was honestly convinced I could fix the whole department morale issue at work if people would just follow my plan, and also somehow fix our family routines at home—like meals, chores, mornings, everything. I had charts in my head. Well, not just in my head, actually, I started making one, and then got distracted because the printer jammed, which always makes me irrationally angry.\n\nBut yes, I felt more capable than usual. Like I could just… organize everyone into being better.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… apparently I was. I didn’t totally hear it that way at the time, you know? In my head I was just explaining things efficiently.\n\nBut my husband said I was talking really fast, and a couple coworkers told me I kept jumping in before they could finish. Like I was sort of barreling through the conversation. I remember thinking they were being a little slow, which sounds rude now.\n\nIt’s funny, because I usually try to be careful in meetings—manager brain, let everyone have space, all that—but during that stretch I guess I was… not doing that. They said they couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Not serious-serious, I don’t think. I mean, I’m still showing up. I’m going to work, I’m getting the main things done, and at home I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut… it has affected things. I’ve been slower making decisions at work, which is awkward as a manager because people are sort of waiting on me. And at home I’ve been less reliable with family plans — like I’ll say we’re going to do something and then I’m not really prepared, or I lose track of it.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled back from the art stuff I usually do, which maybe sounds unrelated, but it’s one of the things that keeps me connected to people. I haven’t been showing up for that as much. So, not like everything fell apart, but it’s definitely made me less… steady.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that energized period last?\n\nPatient: The energized part… I’m trying to count it cleanly. It wasn’t just one weekend or something. The whole swing, from being really driven and keyed up into being low, has been about a month now.\n\nThe energized stretch itself felt like it ran for days in a row—maybe I’d need to look at my calendar to be exact, because I always think I’ll remember and then I don’t. My calendar is a mess, too, which is funny because I keep telling everyone else to use shared calendars. But overall, this has been going on around a month.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized period last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… I mean, I’m not great at pinning down the exact start and stop. It kind of blended together.\n\nBut this whole recent swing — from being unusually energized and driven and then dropping into feeling low — has been going on for about a month. The energized part was definitely more than just a day or two. I’d have to check my calendar to be precise, though, assuming I actually wrote it down, which… lately has been questionable.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you have any hallucinations or delusional beliefs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I didn’t see or hear things that weren’t there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDelusional beliefs is harder for me to answer, because when I’m in that mode I can be very convinced my way of seeing things is the right one — but that’s also sort of my manager brain, you know? Like I can get overly sure about a plan or a decision, and then later I’m like, okay, maybe I was being a little much.\n\nBut not like… believing someone was spying on me or messages were being sent to me or anything like that. Nothing that extreme.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, were you hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t hospitalized for it. It didn’t get to that point.\n\nI mean, I was still going to work and doing my regular stuff, though probably in a more intense way than usual. But no ER, no inpatient stay or anything like that. I’ve only really dealt with hospitals for, like, family stuff and routine things, and honestly parking alone is enough to make me avoid them. But no, not because of that period."
